## ⛔ Blocking: a source contradicts the course (3)

- **c-m01-18** (claim) — contradicted: “Skin was not an endpoint in these mouse studies.”
  - `baker-2016-p16-clearance-mice` (full text): “iWAT cell diameter, adipose depot weight, skin thickness, and skeletal muscle diameter measurements were as described 4.”
  - Skin thickness, cutaneous wound healing, and skin fibrosis were explicitly evaluated as endpoints.
- **c-m04-09** (claim) — contradicted: “For Leuphasyl (pentapeptide-18), no independent or indexed human trial exists, with efficacy data limited to manufacturer studies.”
  - `errante-2020-cosmeceutical-peptides` (full text): “Both instrumental and volunteer-involving studies acknowledge its effectiveness in wrinkles reduction (Dragomirescu et al., 2014 ).”
  - Errante et al. cite a volunteer-involving study (Dragomirescu et al., 2014) acknowledging wrinkle reduction for Leuphasyl.
- **m1-p15** (block) — contradicted: “No randomized trial evaluating senotherapies with clinical skin-ageing outcomes exists.”
  - `chung-2019-topical-rapamycin` (full text): “To determine whether rapamycin, an FDA-approved drug targeting the mechanistic target of rapamycin (mTOR) complex, can reduce senescence and markers of aging in human skin, an exploratory, placebo-controlled, interventional trial was conducted in a clinical dermatology setting.”
  - Chung 2019 was an exploratory prospective randomized placebo-controlled trial evaluating topical rapamycin on both tissue markers and clinical skin-aging outcomes such as wrinkling and hand grading.

## ⚠ Major: overstated, disputed, or absent from a source read or searched in full (30)

- **c-m01-12** (claim) — partial: “Receptor presence is only an initial hypothesis-generating step that requires subsequent validation and clinical trials for efficacy.”
  - `hormone-cell-atlas-portal` (landing page (little more than a title)): “Important: these predictions are based on transcriptomic data and should be considered as putative and hypothesis-generating, rather than as functional hormones and receptors. The user takes responsibility for validation using appropriate methods (e.g. in vitro, in vivo) and for describing appropriately their use of transcriptome-based scores and predictions.”
  - The source states that transcriptomic predictions of hormone receptors are putative and hypothesis-generating requiring validation (such as in vitro or in vivo), but does not specify human clinical trials.
- **c-m02-04** (claim) — partial: “The guideline recommends taking a drug history covering all glucocorticoid routes (oral, inhaled, topical, injected, herbal tonics) to exclude exogenous use before any biochemical testing.”
  - `nieman-2008-cushings-diagnosis-guideline` (abstract only): “After excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use, we recommend testing for Cushing's syndrome in patients with multiple and progressive features compatible with the syndrome, particularly those with a high discriminatory value, and patients with adrenal incidentaloma.”
  - The abstract recommends excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use before testing, but does not detail the specific administration routes or products.
- **c-m02-05** (claim) — partial: “The guideline recommends testing patients with multiple and progressive features (particularly the discriminating ones) or features unusual for age.”
  - `nieman-2008-cushings-diagnosis-guideline` (abstract only): “After excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use, we recommend testing for Cushing's syndrome in patients with multiple and progressive features compatible with the syndrome, particularly those with a high discriminatory value, and patients with adrenal incidentaloma.”
  - The abstract supports testing patients with multiple and progressive features with high discriminatory value, but does not mention features unusual for age.
- **c-m02-11** (claim) — not found: “The FDA labeling changes did not add skin as an indication.”
  - `fda-mht-labeling-approval-2026-02-12` (web page (the cited page itself))
  - The provided FDA sources do not mention skin or skin-related indications.
- **c-m02-18** (claim) — partial: “The Endocrine Society recommends testing acutely ill patients with signs of primary adrenal insufficiency and treating a suspected adrenal crisis before test results are available.”
  - `bornstein-2016-pai-guideline` (abstract only): “We recommend diagnostic tests for the exclusion of primary adrenal insufficiency in all patients with indicative clinical symptoms or signs. In particular, we suggest a low diagnostic (and therapeutic) threshold in acutely ill patients, as well as in patients with predisposing factors.”
  - The guideline abstract recommends diagnostic testing for patients with indicative symptoms and suggests a low diagnostic and therapeutic threshold in acutely ill patients, but does not explicitly state treating before test results are available.
- **c-m04-11** (claim) — partial: “BPC-157 evidence is primarily composed of an extensive rodent literature, largely from one research group, alongside uncontrolled human case series.”
  - `mcguire-2025-bpc157-regeneration-or-risk` (full text): “Despite broad preclinical support, human data are extremely limited. Only three pilot studies have examined BPC-157 in humans, including its use for intraarticular knee pain, interstitial cystitis, and intravenous safety/pharmacokinetics.”
  - Preclinical animal evidence and limited uncontrolled human studies are confirmed, but the sources do not explicitly state that the literature is largely from one research group.
- **c-m05-17** (claim) — partial: “PTD-DBM fuses a cell-penetrating TAT fragment to a sequence that competes CXXC5 off Dishevelled, activating Wnt/β-catenin.”
  - `lee-2017-ptd-dbm-cxxc5-hair` (abstract only): “Disrupting the CXXC5-Dishevelled interaction with a competitor peptide activated the Wnt/β-catenin pathway and accelerated hair regrowth and wound-induced hair follicle neogenesis.”
  - The abstract confirms that a competitor peptide disrupting the CXXC5-Dishevelled interaction activates Wnt/β-catenin, but does not name PTD-DBM or specify the TAT fusion.
- **c-m06-07** (claim) — partial: “GH therapy in healthy older adults causes more oedema, arthralgia, carpal tunnel syndrome, and gynaecomastia, and a trend to diabetes.”
  - `liu-2007-gh-healthy-elderly` (abstract only): “The literature published on randomized, controlled trials evaluating GH therapy in the healthy elderly is limited but suggests that it is associated with small changes in body composition and increased rates of adverse events.”
  - The abstract mentions increased adverse events in general and diabetes/glucose intolerance, but does not list oedema, arthralgia, carpal tunnel syndrome, or gynaecomastia.
- **c-m06-13** (claim) — partial: “Ipamorelin's only efficacy RCT (postoperative ileus, phase 2) showed no benefit over placebo.”
  - `beck-2014-ipamorelin-ileus-rct` (abstract only): “There were no significant differences between ipamorelin and placebo in the key and secondary efficacy analyses.”
  - The trial was a phase 2 RCT in postoperative ileus showing no significant efficacy differences versus placebo, but the abstract does not confirm it was the only efficacy RCT.
- **c-m07-16** (claim) — partial: “A seized injection vial held an undocumented GHRP-2 analogue identifiable only by high-resolution mass spectrometry.”
  - `poplawska-2019-ghrp2-analogue-vial` (abstract only): “Therefore, in the present work, a qualitative analysis by liquid chromatography coupled to high-resolution tandem mass spectrometry with a quadrupole time-of-flight analyzer was performed to identify a new heptapeptide (MW = 874.02 Da) - a glycine analogue of GHRP-2.”
  - The study identified a novel glycine analogue of GHRP-2 in a seized vial using high-resolution tandem mass spectrometry, but does not state it is the only method capable of identification.
- **m1-p05›interaction** (unit) — partial: “UVB turns 7-dehydrocholesterol into vitamin D3, which the blood carries to liver and kidney”
  - `zouboulis-2004-skin-endocrine-gland` (full text): “The skin is the unique site of cholecalciferol production and the liver is thought to be the main site of conversion to 25(OH)D 3 .”
  - The source confirms UVB-driven synthesis of vitamin D3 from 7-dehydrocholesterol and its release into the circulation to the liver, but does not mention transport to the kidney.
- **m1-p05›interaction** (unit) — partial: “Scalp follicles convert testosterone to DHT, which shrinks susceptible follicles”
  - `slominski-2013-skin-steroidogenesis` (full text): “Very high doses of T or DHT induce apoptosis of DPCs through the bcl-2 pathway.”
  - The sources describe 5-alpha reductase conversion of testosterone to DHT and DHT-induced dermal papilla cell apoptosis, but do not specifically use the term 'shrinks susceptible follicles'.
- **m2-p03** (block) — partial: “Local cortisol is set inside the skin independently of systemic levels, so blood, saliva, or hair cortisol cannot measure it.”
  - `tiganescu-2011-11bhsd1-skin` (abstract only): “Within target tissues, GC availability is regulated at a prereceptor level, independently of systemic levels, by isozymes of 11β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase (11β-HSD) that interconvert active cortisol and inactive cortisone.”
  - Tiganescu 2011 states glucocorticoid availability is regulated within target tissues independently of systemic levels, but does not explicitly mention saliva or hair testing.
- **m2-p19›interaction** (unit) — partial: “Clinicians should take a steroid history to exclude exogenous glucocorticoid use, including creams, skin-lighteners, and injections.”
  - `nieman-2008-cushings-diagnosis-guideline` (abstract only): “After excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use, we recommend testing for Cushing's syndrome in patients with multiple and progressive features compatible with the syndrome, particularly those with a high discriminatory value, and patients with adrenal incidentaloma.”
  - The guideline recommends excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use, but the provided abstract does not list specific formulations such as creams, skin-lighteners, or injections.
- **m2-p19›interaction** (unit) — partial: “Clinicians should check for high-discriminator features of Cushing's syndrome, including bruising, plethora, proximal weakness, and wide purple striae.”
  - `nieman-2008-cushings-diagnosis-guideline` (abstract only): “After excluding exogenous glucocorticoid use, we recommend testing for Cushing's syndrome in patients with multiple and progressive features compatible with the syndrome, particularly those with a high discriminatory value, and patients with adrenal incidentaloma.”
  - The guideline recommends testing in patients with features of high discriminatory value, but the specific physical features are not detailed in the abstract.
- **m2-p19›interaction** (unit) — partial: “Hormone therapy indications should be tied to menopausal symptoms and bone preservation, never skin alone.”
  - `nams-2022-hormone-therapy-position-statement` (abstract only): “Hormone therapy remains the most effective treatment for vasomotor symptoms (VMS) and the genitourinary syndrome of menopause and has been shown to prevent bone loss and fracture.”
  - The position statement confirms indications are for vasomotor/genitourinary symptoms and bone loss prevention, but the explicit exclusion of skin alone is not stated in the abstract.
- **m4-p15** (block) — partial: “Both clinical trials of LL-37 were run by the developer.”
  - `mahlapuu-2021-ll37-phase2b` (full text): “The study was financed by Promore Pharma AB, Solna, Sweden.”
  - The provided text confirms developer financing and employment for the 2021 trial, but contains no funding or developer information for the 2014 trial.
- **m5-p02** (block) — partial: “Pattern hair loss is mediated by dihydrotestosterone (converted from testosterone by 5-alpha-reductase) shrinking susceptible scalp follicles.”
  - `adil-2017-aga-treatments-meta` (abstract only): “Androgenetic alopecia, or male pattern hair loss, is a hair loss disorder mediated by dihydrotestosterone, the potent form of testosterone.”
  - The source confirms androgenetic alopecia is mediated by dihydrotestosterone, but does not detail 5-alpha-reductase conversion or follicular miniaturization.
- **m5-p15** (block) — partial: “The 2017 study by Lee et al. studied PTD-DBM in mice and human scalp tissue, and its senior author is affiliated with a biotech company.”
  - `lee-2017-ptd-dbm-cxxc5-hair` (abstract only): “CXXC5 was upregulated in miniaturized hair follicles and arrector pili muscles in human balding scalps. The inhibitory effects of CXXC5 on alkaline phosphatase activity and cell proliferation were demonstrated using human hair follicle dermal papilla cells. Moreover, CXXC5 -/- mice displayed accelerated hair regrowth”
  - The abstract mentions mice and human scalp tissues/cells, but author affiliations are omitted from the provided text.
- **m5-p16›interaction** (unit) — partial: “PTD-DBM spray lacks human clinical trial evidence.”
  - `lee-2017-ptd-dbm-cxxc5-hair` (abstract only): “Disrupting the CXXC5-Dishevelled interaction with a competitor peptide activated the Wnt/β-catenin pathway and accelerated hair regrowth and wound-induced hair follicle neogenesis. Overall, these findings suggest that the CXXC5-Dishevelled interaction is a potential target for the treatment of hair loss.”
  - The cited study evaluates CXXC5 inhibition in mouse models and cultured human dermal papilla cells rather than in human clinical trials, but the excerpt does not broadly confirm the absence of all human trials.
- **m5-check** (unit) — partial: “Melanotan II human evidence includes two tiny 1990s studies and case reports of harm.”
  - `dorr-1996-melanotan-ii-pilot` (abstract only): “A single-blind, alternating day (saline or MT-II), placebo-controlled trial was conducted in 3 normal male volunteers at the starting dose of 0.01 mg/kg of MT-II.”
  - The sources include a 1996 pilot study in 3 volunteers and case reports of harm, but do not document a second 1990s trial.
- **m5-check** (unit) — partial: “Melanotan II is approved nowhere, with US, UK and Australian regulators on record against it.”
  - `bohm-2025-mc1r-activation-review` (full text): “Melanotan II is a synthetic cyclic α‐MSH analogue that is a nonselective MCR agonist and is not approved for use in any therapeutic indication.”
  - The provided sources confirm Melanotan II is unapproved and that US and UK regulators warn against it, but the Australian regulator source is unavailable.
- **m6-p09** (block) — partial: “There are no randomized trials evaluating a skin outcome for tesamorelin, CJC-1295, ipamorelin, or IGF-1 LR3.”
  - `dominikowski-2026-gh-axis-peptides-review` (full text): “At the opposite end, CJC-1295 without DAC, PEG-MGF, and IGF-1 LR3 (tier D) have no peer-reviewed human studies and are supported only by preclinical extrapolation and grey-literature user narratives. Most other GHRH analogues and ghrelin-mimetic secretagogues occupy an intermediate tier (B), with phase I/II human studies that do not address performance- or body-composition endpoints”
  - Dominikowski 2026 reviewed the human evidence through January 2026 (rather than September 2026) and confirms that none of these agents have randomized trials with skin outcomes.
- **cq-m05-1** (question) — partial: “Melanotan II has no approved indications and regulators in the US, UK, and Australia have issued safety warnings about it.”
  - `mhra-foi-24-274-melanotan-ii` (pdf (full document)): “These products may cause serious side effects, so our advice to those who have used Melanotan II injections or nasal sprays is to stop using them immediately and if you have suffered side effects, speak to your doctor and report them to the MHRA through our Yellow Card Scheme.”
  - US and UK regulatory warnings and unapproved status are documented, but the Australian TGA advisory source is unavailable.
- **cq-m06-2** (question) — partial: “Trials in healthy older adults show growth hormone therapy causes more oedema, joint pain, and glucose problems.”
  - `blackman-2002-gh-sex-steroids-rct` (abstract only): “Because adverse effects were frequent (importantly, diabetes and glucose intolerance), GH interventions in the elderly should be confined to controlled studies.”
  - The provided text documents frequent adverse effects including diabetes and glucose intolerance, but does not specifically list oedema or joint pain.
- **tutor-m02-hrt-skin** (tutor) — partial: “The largest trial on facial skin (485 women, 48 weeks) and a four-year trial in recently menopausal women found no difference in wrinkles.”
  - `phillips-2008-ht-facial-skin-rct` (abstract only): “Four hundred eighty-five subjects were enrolled in this 48-week randomized, double-blind study.”
  - Phillips 2008 supports the 485-woman, 48-week trial finding no significant difference in wrinkling, but the four-year trial is not in the provided texts.
- **tutor-m02-hrt-skin** (tutor) — partial: “For skin, daily sunscreen and a prescription retinoid have better evidence than hormone therapy.”
  - `hughes-2013-sunscreen-skin-aging-rct` (abstract only): “The daily sunscreen group showed no detectable increase in skin aging after 4.5 years. Skin aging from baseline to the end of the trial was 24% less in the daily sunscreen group than in the discretionary sunscreen group (relative odds, 0.76 [95% CI, 0.59 to 0.98]).”
  - Hughes 2013 shows daily sunscreen retards skin aging, but prescription retinoids are not mentioned in the provided text.
- **tutor-m05-melanotan** (tutor) — partial: “FDA (April 2026), MHRA (April 2024) and TGA (August 2026) have all put warnings on record regarding Melanotan II.”
  - `habbema-2017-msh-analogue-risks` (abstract only): “Multiple national health organizations have issued safety warnings regarding the use of melanotan I and II.”
  - The FDA page (April 2026) lists safety risks and Habbema notes multiple national health organizations have issued warnings, but specific MHRA and TGA dates are not in the provided texts.
- **tutor-m05-hair-peptides** (tutor) — partial: “Approved options minoxidil, finasteride in men, and baricitinib for severe alopecia areata carry grade A evidence.”
  - `adil-2017-aga-treatments-meta` (abstract only): “Currently, minoxidil and finasteride are Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved, and HairMax LaserComb, which is FDA-cleared, are the only treatments recognized by the FDA as treatments of androgenetic alopecia.”
  - The meta-analysis confirms FDA approval of minoxidil and finasteride for androgenetic alopecia, but does not address baricitinib or evidence grades.
- **tutor-m06-gh-excess** (tutor) — partial: “Signs of growth-hormone excess include puffy, thickened skin of the face, hands and feet, oily skin, more sweating, skin tags, acanthosis nigricans, tight rings or shoes, night-time tingling and carpal tunnel symptoms.”
  - `ben-shlomo-2006-acromegaly-skin` (abstract only): “Skin puffiness due to dermal glycosaminoglycan accumulation and edema are most prominent in the face, hands and feet. Oily skin with large pores, hypertrichosis, and excessive sweating are common features. Pigmented skin tags, acanthosis nigricans, and psoriasis are also encountered.”
  - The cited text confirms cutaneous changes including puffiness of face/hands/feet, oily skin, sweating, skin tags, and acanthosis nigricans, but does not mention tight rings/shoes or night-time tingling.

## · Minor: needs a human with the full text (260)

- **c-m01-02** (claim) — not found: “Evidence for cutaneous steroidogenesis rests mainly on cell, tissue, and biochemical studies.”
  - cited: `slominski-2013-skin-steroidogenesis` (fulltext), `zouboulis-2004-skin-endocrine-gland` (fulltext), `hannah-shmouni-2026-are` (none)
  - While the cited reviews detail numerous in vitro, tissue, and biochemical assays, the provided text does not contain a summary statement characterizing the overall evidence base as resting mainly on these study types.

## Supported (for the record)

- **c-m01-01** — “The skin functions as a peripheral neuro-immuno-endocrine organ composed of four immuno-endocrine units (epidermal, dermal, hypodermal, adn…” ← `slominski-2025-skin-neuro-immuno-endocrinology`: “The skin neuro–immuno–endocrine system is composed of epidermal, dermal, hypodermal and adnexal immune–endocrine units, the activities of which are coordinated by sensory nerves.” · “The neuro-immuno-endocrine model of the skin is a mechanistic framework validated and supported experimentally across cell, tissue, and ani…” ← `slominski-2025-skin-neuro-immuno-endocrinology`: “The original hypothesis that the skin is a neuroendocrine organ that secretes neuroendocrine and immune mediators to regulate local responses against environmental stress with systemic implications 17 , 45 has been vali…”
- **c-m01-02** — “Skin cells carry the enzymes to make steroids from cholesterol (such as CYP11A1) and to activate circulating precursors (such as 11β-HSD1, …” ← `slominski-2013-skin-steroidogenesis`: “Most recently it has been established that skin cells contain the entire biochemical apparatus necessary for production of glucocorticoids, androgens and estrogens either from precursors of systemic origin or, alternati…”
- **c-m01-03** — “Human scalp hair follicles kept in organ culture respond to CRH by making POMC products and secreting cortisol.” ← `ito-2005-hair-follicle-hpa`: “We show that microdissected, organ-cultured human scalp hair follicles respond to CRH stimulation by up-regulating proopiomelanocortin (POMC) transcription and immunoreactivity (IR) for ACTH and alpha-MSH, which must ha…” · “Hair follicles down-regulate their own CRH in response to glucocorticoids, operating an HPA-like feedback loop without systemic connections.” ← `ito-2005-hair-follicle-hpa`: “Thus, even in the absence of endocrine, neural, or vascular systemic connections, normal human scalp hair follicles directly respond to CRH stimulation in a strikingly similar manner to what is seen in the classical HPA…”
- **c-m01-04** — “In mice, deleting glucocorticoid synthesis in keratinocytes causes spontaneous skin inflammation.” ← `phan-2021-keratinocyte-glucocorticoids`: “In line with 11β-hydroxylase deficiency in human inflammatory skin disorders, genetic in vivo Cyp11b1 ablation and long-term GC deficiency in keratinocytes primed the murine skin immune system resulting in spontaneous s…” · “In mice, keratinocyte glucocorticoid deficiency exacerbates experimental dermatitis models.” ← `phan-2021-keratinocyte-glucocorticoids`: “Deficient skin GC in experimental models for inflammatory skin disorders led to exacerbated contact hypersensitivity and psoriasiform skin inflammation accompanied by decreased regulatory T cells and the involvement of …” · “Keratinocyte-synthesized glucocorticoids control skin immune homeostasis and set local immune tone.” ← `phan-2021-keratinocyte-glucocorticoids`: “Keratinocytes control tissue homeostasis and skin inflammation via immunosuppressive glucocorticoids.” · “Steroidogenic enzyme deficiency and dysregulated skin glucocorticoid synthesis are associated with human inflammatory skin disorders.” ← `phan-2021-keratinocyte-glucocorticoids`: “These results confirm previous studies indicating that epidermal steroidogenesis is dysregulated in prevalent inflammatory skin disorders, such as AD and psoriasis ( 17 , 18 ).”
- **c-m01-05** — “The skin operates a shortened thyroid-like pathway involving TRH, TSH receptors, and deiodinase-generated T3, with mechanistic evidence der…” ← `mancino-2021-thyroid-epidermis`: “Surprisingly, the expression of fully functional proteins typical of the HPT axis and in particular of the TSH and TRH receptors has been found in human skin and in HFs [ 27 ]. Receptors for TSH and TRH in the skin indu…”
- **c-m01-06** — “UVB radiation converts 7-dehydrocholesterol into previtamin D3 in human skin.” ← `bikle-2014-vitamin-d`: “D3 (cholecalciferol) is produced from 7-dehydrocholesterol (7-DHC) through a two-step process in which the B ring is broken by UV light (spectrum 280–320 UVB) radiation from the sun, forming pre-D3 that isomerizes to D3…” · “Vitamin D3 is transported into the bloodstream and hydroxylated sequentially to 25(OH)D and calcitriol (1,25(OH)2D).” ← `bikle-2014-vitamin-d`: “Vitamin D is metabolized first to 25 hydroxyvitamin D (25OHD), then to the hormonal form 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D (1,25(OH)2D).”
- **c-m01-07** — “Reviews describe a cutaneous contribution to circulating androgens and estrogens, particularly in older individuals.” ← `zouboulis-2004-skin-endocrine-gland`: “Human skin produces, activates or inactivates metabolically numerous hormones which are probably important for skin functions but also for functions of the entire human organism, such as sex hormones, especially in aged…” · “Evidence for cutaneous production and systemic contribution of active steroids is established through biochemical apparatus and steroidogen…” ← `slominski-2013-skin-steroidogenesis`: “Most recently it has been established that skin cells contain the entire biochemical apparatus necessary for production of glucocorticoids, androgens and estrogens either from precursors of systemic origin or, alternati…”
- **c-m01-08** — “Hypothyroidism causes dry, coarse skin and myxoedema.” ← `safer-2011-thyroid-skin`: “Generalized myxedema is still the classic cutaneous sign of hypothyroidism. It is caused by deposition of dermal acid mucopolysaccharides, notably hyaluronic acid.”
- **c-m01-09** — “The Hormone Cell Atlas analysed RNA for 379 hormone and receptor genes in about 14 million single cells and nuclei across 47 human tissues,…” ← `fei-2026-hormone-cell-atlas`: “Drawing inspiration from the Human Cell Atlas, we analyzed the expression of 379 hormone and receptor genes in a transcriptomic dataset comprising 14 million single cells and nuclei across 47 human tissues. Using hormon…”
- **c-m01-10** — “The Hormone Cell Atlas authors describe its scores as putative and hypothesis-generating rather than functional hormones and receptors, lea…” ← `hormone-cell-atlas-portal`: “Important: these predictions are based on transcriptomic data and should be considered as putative and hypothesis-generating, rather than as functional hormones and receptors. The user takes responsibility for validatio…” · “Transcript levels alone often fail to predict protein levels.” ← `liu-2016-mrna-protein`: “We conclude that transcript levels by themselves are not sufficient to predict protein levels in many scenarios and to thus explain genotype-phenotype relationships and that high-quality data quantifying different level…”
- **c-m01-13** — “A systematic review found that sunscreen blocks vitamin D synthesis under artificial UV radiation.” ← `neale-2019-sunscreen-vitamin-d`: “In the experimental studies sunscreen use considerably abrogated the vitamin D 3 or 25(OH)D production induced by exposure to artificially generated UVR.” · “Randomized field trials of daily moderate-SPF sunscreen found no reduction in 25(OH)D levels.” ← `neale-2019-sunscreen-vitamin-d`: “The randomized controlled field trials found no effect of daily sunscreen application, but the sunscreens used had moderate protection [sun protection factor SPF) ~16].” · “High-SPF sunscreen products have not been evaluated in trials regarding vitamin D levels.” ← `neale-2019-sunscreen-vitamin-d`: “However, there have been no trials of the high-SPF sunscreens that are now widely recommended.”
- **c-m01-14** — “Senescent cells are permanently growth-arrested.” ← `wang-dreesen-2018-senescence-biomarkers`: “Cellular senescence is an irreversible growth arrest that occurs as a result of different damaging stimuli, including DNA damage, telomere shortening and dysfunction or oncogenic stress.” · “The senescence-associated secretory phenotype (including IL-6, IL-8, matrix metalloproteinases, and growth factors) was characterised in cu…” ← `wang-dreesen-2018-senescence-biomarkers`: “For instance, senescent cells modulate their environment by secreting inflammatory cytokines, chemokines, matrix metalloproteinases (MMPs) and growth factors, collectively known as the senescence-associated secretory ph…”
- **c-m01-15** — “In human skin, cells carrying the senescence marker p16INK4a are more numerous with age in both epidermis and dermis.” ← `ressler-2006-p16-human-skin`: “The number of p16INK4A positive cells was found to be increased in both epidermis and dermis, compartments with strictly different proliferative activities.” · “No single universal marker identifies senescent cells, so counts depend on the marker used.” ← `wang-dreesen-2018-senescence-biomarkers`: “However, a significant hurdle in the aging field has been the identification of a universal biomarker that facilitates the unequivocal detection and quantification of senescent cell types in vitro and in vivo .”
- **c-m01-16** — “In 178 adults of the Leiden Longevity Study, more p16-positive cells in sun-protected arm skin were associated with ageing-type elastic fib…” ← `waaijer-2016-p16-wrinkles`: “The p16INK4a positive epidermal cells (identified as primarily melanocytes) were also significantly associated with more facial wrinkles and a higher perceived age.” · “Participants in the lowest tertile of epidermal p16 counts looked about 3 years younger than those in the highest tertile.” ← `waaijer-2016-p16-wrinkles`: “Participants in the lowest tertile of epidermal p16INK4a counts looked 3 years younger than those in the highest tertile, independently of chronological age and elastic fiber morphology.”
- **c-m01-17** — “Skin collagen falls with age.” ← `shuster-1975-skin-collagen-age`: “Skin collagen decreased with age and was less in the females at all ages.” · “Aged human dermis shows increased fibroblast MMP-1 and fragmented collagen.” ← `fisher-2009-collagen-fragmentation`: “We report here that dermal fibroblasts express increased levels of collagen-degrading matrix metalloproteinases-1 (MMP-1) in aged (>80 years old) compared with young (21 to 30 years old) human skin in vivo.” · “Aged human dermis shows fibroblasts under less mechanical tension that produce less procollagen.” ← `varani-2006-aged-fibroblast-collagen`: “The reduction in collagen synthesis due to fibroblast aging was demonstrated by a lower in vitro production of type I procollagen by dermal fibroblasts isolated from skin of young (18 to 29 years) versus old (80+ years)…” · “Culture work demonstrates that collagen fragmentation feeds back to elevate MMP-1.” ← `fisher-2009-collagen-fragmentation`: “Furthermore, fibroblasts cultured in fragmented collagen lattices express elevated levels of MMP-1, AP-1, and alpha2beta1 integrin.”
- **c-m01-18** — “In mice, clearing p16-positive cells extended healthy lifespan.” ← `baker-2016-p16-clearance-mice`: “Here we show that compared to vehicle alone, AP20187 treatment extended median lifespan in both male and female mice of two distinct genetic backgrounds.” · “Senolytics improved physical function and survival in old mice.” ← `xu-2018-senolytics-mice`: “Moreover, intermittent oral administration of senolytics to both senescent cell-transplanted younger and naturally-aged mice alleviated physical dysfunction and increased post-treatment survival by 36% while reducing mo…”

Severity: *blocking* = a verified passage contradicts the course; *major* = overstated (partial), panel-disputed, or not found in a source that was read in full (likely misattribution); *minor* = not found in an abstract, excerpt or landing page, no verbatim passage, or no readable source text: a human with the full text decides. Fix content at its stage (authoring/source lock), never in this report.
